- [ERGONOMIC-INTEGRATION-1] Late exports, inferred generic calls and unit procedures compose across module boundaries, preserving cross-module effect and result obligations.
- [GENERIC-INFERENCE-1] Generic function calls may omit their entire type-argument list when checked call arguments and the expected result type uniquely determine every type and const length parameter. Matching follows direct variables, fixed arrays, nominal applications, and callback signatures; repeated occurrences must agree. Abstract callers retain distinct symbolic identities and declared requirements. Argument expressions are checked and evaluated once in their original order; ordinary type, effect, ownership, and structural checks remain in force. Constraints alone, defaults, and partial explicit lists do not infer missing arguments; ambiguity and conflicting inference report E_GENERIC_INFERENCE. Unmaterialized phantom type arguments require explicit specialization.
## Unit procedures

- [UNIT-1] The builtin `unit` is a distinct no-value type. Unit functions accept `return;` and implicit fallthrough; `main` still returns `i64`.
- [UNIT-2] Ordinary standalone expressions must have type `unit`; all other values require `_ =` or result handling, including generic bodies. VM calls preserve unit identity and ownership cleanup.
- [UNIT-3] Unit is described explicitly in public schemas and is rejected by data/wire and host ABI boundaries.
- [UNIT-4] Unit fallthrough and early return release owned parameters through the ordinary cleanup paths.
- [UNIT-5] Fallible values with unit success payloads retain all result obligations; a successful `try` yielding unit may appear as a standalone statement.
- [UNIT-6] Public unit-returning functions remain callable within language modules. Their schema uses `kind: unit`; copied VM/host/native ABI invocation rejects unit-containing signatures explicitly instead of interpreting unit as a scalar or capability.

## Managed tracing heap

- [GC-ROOTS-1] Managed list aliases share stable identity and mutations. Retained values, including intermediate call arguments and ordinary aggregate snapshots, root managed objects across allocation and stress collection.
- [GC-CYCLES-1] Nonmoving tracing collection reclaims unreachable cycles, including cycles through ordinary aggregate fields and cycles whose last root left a block, and preserves reachable cycles.
- [GC-PACING-1] The tracing collector's pause interval grows with the live set and keeps a floor: after a collection the next one runs once the young generation has grown by half the surviving bytes or four mebibytes, whichever is larger (the first runs at one mebibyte), and that collection is a full one once the old generation — the bytes the last collection kept — reaches twice the bytes the last full collection kept plus four mebibytes, so a steady allocation workload re-marks its long-lived set a few times instead of at every collection. Collection timing is not observable — the language has no destructors and no program-visible collection counter, and `--gc-stress` still collects at every allocation — and the byte budget is still enforced on every allocation, so peak memory stays within the configured ceiling.
- [GC-OBJECT-1] A tracing-heap object carries a two-word header immediately before its payload: a mark word holding bit 0, the immortal-literal flag, the element count and the large-object flag (bit 63), then the element descriptor. The count stays in the header because precise tracing needs it for any payload address, including one first reached through a root. A large object — one in a size class above 8 KiB, or an oversized one with a segment of its own — keeps the collector's mark in bit 0; every other object's mark is a bit of its segment's side bitmap, and its bit 0 only records that it is old, for the store barrier. The collector's marking worklist is a heap-side array grown on demand; a refused reservation abandons the collection unmarked and refuses the allocation that ran it, so a partly marked graph is never swept. The payload stays aligned to `max_align_t` behind the two words. An arena payload carries the same two words, with the arena flag and its depth in the mark word.
- [GC-SEGMENT-1] Tracing-heap storage is reserved in size-classed segments instead of one host allocation per object. An object's charge is its header plus payload rounded to the payload's 16-byte alignment, then up to a class stride. A class of at most 8 KiB lives in 64 KiB segments aligned to their size and carved from larger host reservations, so an object finds its segment's mark bits by masking its own address; a larger class uses 64 KiB slabs, and an oversized object a one-slot segment of its own. A class hands out storage from holes — runs of free slots a sweep found — and from the never-used, zero-filled tail of its newest segment; a reused slot's payload is zeroed before the program sees it, except a coallocated text block's (GC-NOZERO-1). The sweep reads a segment's mark bits, not its slots: the runs between consecutive live objects become holes, and only the first slot of each receives a hole header. A one-slot oversized segment whose object died returns whole to the host. The byte budget charges the class stride rather than the exact request, refuses an allocation that would pass the limit after a full collection, and spends retained arena chunks before reporting host failure; a segment's unused tail is uncharged runtime slack.
- [GC-GENERATIONS-1] The tracing collector is generational without moving anything: every object that survives a collection stays marked and is old, and an allocation is young. A minor collection marks only young objects — a reference to an old object is not followed — and reclaims only unreachable young objects, sweeping only the segments storage was handed out from since the last sweep; its extra roots are every old object a later store may have given a young reference: each old object whose descriptor is traced and is not a `class` body (list and dictionary headers, element storage, byte views, which change without a barrier), and each old `class` body the store barrier reported. A full collection clears every mark first and reclaims old garbage too; it runs when the old generation reaches the full threshold, whenever an allocation would otherwise cross the byte budget (so an allocation is refused only after a full collection), after a remembered-set or worklist reservation failed, and for `myl_native_heap_collect`. Under `--gc-stress` every allocation still collects, and every third collection is full, so two minor collections run between full ones. Values, traps, traces and exit status are unchanged.
- [GC-AGING-1] A minor collection promotes a young object only the second time it finds it reachable. The first time the object stays young: it is kept (not reclaimed), its mark is cleared again after the sweep and a per-granule age bit records that it survived once; the next minor collection promotes it if it is still reachable and reclaims it otherwise, so a structure that is live across one minor collection and dies before the next — a tree under construction — is never promoted into the old generation. A young object first reached through an old object (an old list, dictionary or remembered `class` body) is promoted at once, since the old container keeps it. A promoted `class` body that references an object the same collection keeps young is listed as remembered, exactly as if the store barrier had reported it, so the next minor collection still traces that edge; a remembered body stays listed while it references a young object. A full collection promotes every survivor. Only promotion counts toward the full-collection threshold. Values, traps, traces and exit status are unchanged.
- [GC-SPARE-1] A size-classed segment a sweep leaves with no object in it (other than its class's current bump segment) is unlinked from its class and becomes a spare, which the next class of any stride that needs a new segment takes before a new host mapping is made. A spare keeps its pages, so its bytes are stale and every slot handed out from it is cleared the way a reused hole's slot is. Size-classed segments are carved from 2 MiB-aligned private anonymous mappings, which read as zero until written. Values, traps and exit status are unchanged.
- [GC-NOZERO-1] A coallocated block (`myl_native_heap_alloc_block`, the storage of every `str` built at run time) does not clear a reused slot's payload: its caller writes every octet it reads before the next safepoint, and its payload is plain bytes the collector never interprets. An arena block is still carved from zero-filled storage, and every other allocation is still zeroed. Values, traps and exit status are unchanged.
- [GC-POOLSET-1] The collector tells a size-classed object from its address alone — whether it lies in one of the heap's pool mappings — so marking a reference to an object that is already marked, which is most of what a minor collection's rescan of old lists and dictionaries does, reads the object's mark and age bits and not its header. Objects outside the pools (large and oversized objects, arena allocations and frozen literals) are still classified by their header. Values, traps and exit status are unchanged.
- [QBE-GEN-BARRIER-1] A field update of a `class` body that stores a value the collector traces first runs the generational barrier: one load and test of the body's mark bit, and for an old body a call to `myl_native_heap_remember`, which clears the bit and lists the body for the next minor collection, so each old body is listed at most once per collection. A field update that stores only scalars runs no barrier. A generated `class` body descriptor is the only one marked `.cell`; every store into an existing class body is a field update (`update_record`), which is what makes the barrier complete.
- [QBE-INLINE-ALLOC-1] Constructing a `class` whose body plus its two-word header fits a size class of at most 256 bytes allocates inline: the generated code bumps the cursor of the heap's per-class inline window over one prepaid slot, writes the header, zeroes the payload and runs the constructor, with no runtime call and no root record. Only an exhausted window calls `myl_native_heap_alloc_small`, the one safepoint and the only branch that publishes roots; it collects exactly when `myl_native_heap_alloc` would, refuses at the budget exactly where it would, and then opens the class's next window on a hole or fresh storage, charging in advance only as many slots as the budget and the collection trigger both still admit. Every prepaid slot nobody took is discharged before any collection decision, when an arena opens, and by the recount after every collection, so the trigger and the `R_MEMORY_BUDGET` refusal happen exactly where charging one object at a time would put them. The windows stay empty while an arena is open, under `--gc-stress` and during an `escape` copy. In a program that opens arenas, a construction inside one bumps the innermost arena's current chunk instead — the free tail the runtime last allocated from at that depth — writing that depth's arena header; the chunk is zero-filled, and every runtime operation on arena chunks first folds that progress back into the chunk. A larger class body keeps the runtime call.
- [GC-BOUNDARY-1] Managed list operations enforce element types, and an out-of-range read or write raises a located list-bounds trap.
- [GC-SOURCE-1] Managed list aliases in immutable local bindings and helper parameters share mutation without explicit allocator authority.
- [GC-SOURCE-2] Source programs can construct list/record cycles; a later managed allocation collects an unreachable cycle before execution shutdown.
- [GC-SOURCE-3] Managed results held as intermediate call arguments or inside ordinary record snapshots survive collection triggered by later arguments and callee allocations.
- [GC-SOURCE-4] Out-of-bounds managed reads and writes produce located R_LIST_BOUNDS traps and release execution roots and backing.
- [GC-SOURCE-6] `noalloc` rejects transitive managed allocation and list growth; fixed-slot mutation and reads remain permitted.
- [GC-SOURCE-7] Managed element declarations reject owned resources and unchecked results, including such values nested in ordinary aggregates.
- [GC-SOURCE-9] Bytecode validation rejects managed metadata on record shapes that are not managed containers.
- [GC-GENERIC-1] Generic managed list creation, mutation, and identity helpers preserve shared aliases without explicit allocator authority.
- [GC-GENERIC-2] Recursive generic list and record declarations form managed reference graphs without recursive value layout.
- [GC-GENERIC-3] Ordinary directly recursive value declarations remain rejected even when managed lists are available.
- [GC-GENERIC-4] Source formatting preserves generic list declarations and contextual uses of list as a variant name.

## Managed standard library

- [GC-GENERIC-5] Managed list elements cannot contain allocator or provider capabilities, including nested records, arrays, and instantiated generic elements; declarations reject with E_LIST_CAPABILITY.
- [GC-CAPABILITY-1] Managed element declarations reject capability values and capabilities nested in aggregates, preserving the source authority boundary.

- [GC-SOURCE-11] Lexical block, break, and continue exits release managed local roots, including roots nested in copyable records, so unreachable cycles can be reclaimed at the next allocation before the enclosing function returns.

## Managed arenas

- [ARENA-MANAGED-1] Python-style `with arena() as name:` and `with arena():` open a region over ordinary managed objects. Every managed allocation performed in the block's dynamic extent, including allocations inside called helpers, is tagged with that arena's nesting depth; arenas nest and the innermost one wins. Normal block exit releases all storage allocated at that depth in bulk; native runtime counters distinguish arena releases from tracing collection.
- [ARENA-MANAGED-2] `name.escape(value)` deep-copies the part of the managed graph reachable from `value` that lives in the innermost arena into the enclosing region, preserving sharing and cycles inside the copy and sharing objects that already live in an older region. Escape carries the `managed_allocation` effect. The arena binding itself is opaque: it cannot be stored, passed, returned, or compared, only used as the receiver of `escape`, and `escape` must name the innermost active arena.
- [ARENA-ESCAPE-CLASS-1] `escape` copies `class` instances as well as collections: every instance reachable in the dying arena gets one clone in the enclosing region, allocated with the body descriptor and element count its source header records, so the clone is an ordinary collected object (young on the tracing heap, under the class store barrier) or an allocation of the enclosing arena. Sharing and cycles through class fields are preserved, and an instance already in an older region is shared.
- [ARENA-MANAGED-3] Allocation metadata records each managed object's arena depth and every store of a managed reference into a heap object is checked: list append, list slot writes, dictionary key/value writes, and values nested in record snapshots. Storing a reference of a younger arena into an older container traps with a located `R_ARENA_ESCAPE`, including stores performed inside helpers called from the block.
- [ARENA-MANAGED-4] Inside a `with arena()` block the checker rejects, with `E_ARENA_ESCAPE`, assigning a managed-bearing value to a binding declared before the block, returning a managed-bearing value, and direct stores into an outer-declared collection or record (`append`, indexed writes, dictionary writes, and field assignment). A managed-bearing type is any type whose values may hold a garbage-collected reference; scalars, enums, domains and records with only scalar fields are free to escape. `name.escape(value)` is the exception and may also be returned from the outermost arena scope of a function. Diagnostics name the escaping expression, the arena scope, and the outer binding, and render complete JSON provenance.
- [ARENA-MANAGED-5] Normal exit, `return`, `?` propagation, `break`/`continue` out of the block, and traps or host allocation failure unwinding through it all release the arena exactly once. Frame-owned values are released before the bulk free, and at an explicit block exit any binding declared before the block that still reaches arena storage traps with `R_ARENA_ESCAPE` as a defence in depth. An arena scope is always closed by the frame that opened it; a mismatched close is an internal-error rejection.
- [ARENA-MANAGED-6] Tracing collection continues to run at managed allocation safepoints inside an arena, including under `--gc-stress`. Arena allocations remain alive until scope exit and are never individually swept or queued for graph marking; when GC runs, arena contents are scanned for references to older GC objects, which remain live. Automatic collection thresholds count ordinary GC bytes; growing an arena can request collection when needed to satisfy the total memory budget. Collection is suspended only for the duration of one `escape` copy, whose partly built clones are not yet rooted. Allocation inside an arena still contributes the `managed_allocation` effect, so `noalloc` rejects it; entering a scope itself contributes no effect.
- [ARENA-MANAGED-7] Arena scopes, bulk release, and escape copies unwind cleanly on every host allocation failure, leaving no live managed objects at execution teardown.
- [ARENA-MANAGED-8] Managed arena scopes lower on the QBE native backend. Native arena payloads use aligned, zero-filled bump chunks, and each payload's region is read from its own header (ARENA-MANAGED-11). The memory budget charges full chunk reservations including metadata and slack, region exit frees chunks without walking individual objects, collection backing grows in its owner's region, the store barrier is emitted before each managed reference store whose element type can hold a collected reference, `escape` deep-copies into the enclosing region with collection suspended, `end_arena` scans the frame's outer bindings and clears the block's own before the bulk release, and every native frame that opens an arena restores its entry depth on return and while a trap, `?` propagation, `break`/`continue` or host allocation failure unwinds through it. Functions that open no arena emit no depth bookkeeping, while their managed stores retain the program-wide barrier. An arena-free program emits neither barrier nor depth bookkeeping. Generic function bodies reject arena scopes with `E_GENERIC_BODY_UNSUPPORTED`.
- [ARENA-MANAGED-9] Locals are function-scoped, so a managed-bearing binding first assigned inside a `with arena()` block stays visible after the block, but it is confined to the released region: reading it once the arena is gone is a static `E_ARENA_ESCAPE` naming the binding, not a dangling read. Reassigning the name after the block re-homes it to the current region and clears the confinement; a scalar binding first assigned inside the arena carries nothing and is read freely. `end_arena` still clears the block's own managed slots before the bulk release, so no confined root survives into the next frame-root scan.
- [ARENA-MANAGED-10] Bulk release retains a closed scope's chunks for the next scope instead of returning them to the host, so a loop that opens one arena per iteration reserves and zero-fills a chunk once rather than once per iteration. Retention is bounded — at most four chunks, at most 2 MiB, never a chunk above the 1 MiB ordinary growth ceiling, and never more than the memory budget itself — and a chunk past the bound is freed as before. A retained chunk is charged to nothing: release discharges it from the budget, `reserved_bytes` and `chunks_live` exactly as freeing it did, and reuse charges it again exactly as a fresh reservation would, and only when its size is at least the request's minimum and no larger than the size the sizing policy would itself have reserved. So `--max-memory` keeps counting only the bytes serving live allocations, a small budget still shrinks chunks rather than bypassing the limit, exhaustion still names the budget, and a refused host reservation first frees every retained chunk and retries. Reuse zeroes exactly the bytes the previous scope could have written — the headers and payloads its `used` prefix spans — leaving a chunk byte-identical to a freshly zero-filled one, so precise tracing, nested scopes, escape copies, collection under stress and GC objects retained through an arena all behave as before. Statistics separate the two sources: `chunks` counts host reservations, `reused` counts chunks taken back from the cache, and `cached`/`cached_bytes` report what is retained; heap destruction frees the cache.
- [ARENA-MANAGED-11] Every native arena allocation is preceded by the same two-word header as a tracing-heap object. Its mark word records an arena flag, the owning depth and the element count, and its second word the element descriptor. So the store barrier, collection marking and arena scanning, `escape` copies and element lookup find a payload's region and shape with one load, whatever the number of chunks: storing N values inside an arena takes time linear in N. A container already living in the innermost open arena accepts a store without examining the value, since no live object is younger. The barrier traps exactly where it did before. A frozen literal or tracing-heap object never carries the flag, because a tracing-heap allocation holds at most 2^60 − 1 elements. One arena header holds at most 2^38 − 1 elements and depth 4,194,303. A larger arena allocation, or a scope opened at that depth, fails as an allocation failure.
- [ARENA-CHUNKS-1] Each open arena depth keeps its own list of chunks, newest first. An allocation at any depth — the innermost, or an outer one while inner scopes are open — finds its depth's current chunk at the head of that list without looking at any other depth's chunks, and closing a scope frees exactly that depth's list: its cost is the chunks it frees, not every open chunk. Collection scans every depth's chunks as before. Values, traps and exit status are unchanged.

- [SURFACE-STMT-1] A statement ends at its newline, at the dedent that closes its suite, or at EOF; two statements written on one line are separated by `;`.
- [SURFACE-STMT-2] Postfix `?` propagates result errors and unwraps success with postfix precedence.
- [SURFACE-STMT-3] `None` and `()` both spell the unit type and unit value; a bare `return` before a newline or a dedent returns unit.
- [SURFACE-STMT-4] Calls and function parameters permit trailing commas; a delimiter continues an expression across newlines.
- [SURFACE-STMT-5] Parse-only declaration listing and module formatting preserve newline-terminated module, import, export, and type directives.
- [SURFACE-STMT-6] Project discovery recognizes newline-terminated directives and declarations while allowing multiline function signatures.

## Command-line authority

- [CAPABILITY-1] `Filesystem`, `Network`, `Clock`, `Entropy` and `Environment` are the canonical capability types. The removed `*_cap` spellings report `E_REMOVED_SPELLING` with the corresponding type; declared or imported types of the canonical name still win. Authority stays explicit: an entry point declares what it needs by parameter type, helpers receive grants as ordinary arguments, and no numeric cast or copy mints one.
- [CAPABILITY-2] `mylang run` and `run-project` grant every capability by default: filesystem reading and writing with no directory restriction, the clock, entropy and the whole process environment. `--restrict` switches the command to deny-by-default, and only then do `--allow-read=DIR` and `--allow-write=DIR` (each repeatable, up to eight directories), `--allow-clock`, `--allow-random`, `--allow-env` and `--allow-all` narrow anything; without `--restrict` they are accepted and narrow nothing. `--allow-random=SEED` pins the entropy seed in either mode, because it selects a stream rather than withholding one; an unseeded stream is seeded from the wall clock. `--allow-all` grants the clock, entropy, every environment name and read/write access to the working directory, and cannot be combined with another grant. A repeated flag, an empty-valued flag, and any of these options on a command that does not execute a program are usage errors.
- [CAPABILITY-3] Under `--restrict`, an entry point that names a capability the command line did not grant is rejected after checking and lowering but before the first guest instruction, with a message that says `--restrict` is active, names the flag that would grant the capability, and offers to drop `--restrict`; the status is 2. Without `--restrict` that refusal cannot happen. Grants for capabilities the entry point does not request provision providers that are never read.
- [CAPABILITY-4] The default filesystem grant is one direct root over the process working directory: a relative guest path resolves against it and an absolute path names itself, each operation applies the whole path at once and follows symlinks, and write authority is always present, so the default can never be narrower than a read grant. Under `--restrict`, directory grants are an ordered search path of scoped roots instead: a guest path must be relative with no empty, `.`, `..`, NUL or backslash component, it is tried against each granted root in order and the first success wins, traversal opens each component with symlink following disabled and final opens reject symlinks. Modes that change the tree — write, exclusive create, truncate, create directory and remove — consider only roots granted with `--allow-write`, which also grants reading that root, and a read-only grant rejects them before opening anything. Neither mode is a sandbox: the providers run inside the compiler process, the host filesystem tree is trusted, directory handles retain authority across renames, and directory scoping does not distinguish hard links to shared inodes.
- [CAPABILITY-5] The clock reports Unix-epoch nanoseconds from the host wall clock, with no monotonicity guarantee. Entropy is a reproducible pseudorandom stream, seeded by `--allow-random=SEED` when given and otherwise from the wall clock; it is not a cryptographic entropy source, and a program needing one must be embedded with its own provider. The environment exposes the host process environment, restricted to the `--allow-env=NAME,...` allowlist when one is given, reporting absent names as absent and denying names outside the list.
- [CAPABILITY-6] The default filesystem grant carries the process's own authority, so like C's `fopen` it opens whatever the process can open other than a directory: a FIFO, a character device such as `/dev/null`, or the `/dev/fd/N` pipe a shell's process substitution `<(...)` names, and a read waits for the FIFO's writer and reads to end of file; truncation still applies only to a regular file. Under `--restrict`, a granted directory serves regular files only, exactly as before: a FIFO or device inside it is refused with the ordinary denial, opened without waiting for a writer, and absolute paths such as `/dev/fd/N` remain outside every grant.

## Ordinary output

- [IO-1] `stream.print(values...)` writes each argument's Python rendering to the receiver stream's destination, separated by single spaces and followed by one newline. `int` and the fixed-width integers render in decimal, `bool` as `True`/`False`, `None` as `None`, `float` with a `.0` suffix when it has no fractional digits and `nan`/`inf`/`-inf` for the nonfinite values, `str` raw at the top level and `repr`-quoted inside a container, and `bytes` as a Python `b'...'` literal with `\n`, `\r`, `\t`, `\'`, `\\` and `\xNN` escapes. Lists render as `[a, b]`, insertion-ordered dictionaries as `{k: v}` and fixed arrays as `[a, b]`. Nesting deeper than 24 levels renders as `...`, so a cyclic managed graph still terminates. `stream.print()` writes only the newline.
- [IO-2] Records render as `Name(field=value, ...)` in declaration order, enum cases as `Name.case`, union cases as `Name.case(payload)` or `Name.case`, checked results as `Ok(value)`/`Err(value)` and optionals as `Some(value)`/`None`. Module qualification is stripped from the printed name. A record with a moved field prints `<moved>` for that field rather than reading it.
- [IO-3] `stream.print` accepts the keyword arguments `sep=` and `end=`, each an ordinary `str` expression that replaces the separator or the trailing newline. Positional arguments must precede both keywords, neither may be repeated, and any other keyword is rejected with `E_IO_ARGUMENT`. Printed values evaluate in source order, then `sep=`, then `end=`. Both keywords materialize the prelude `str` type on demand, so a program whose only text is a separator still checks, and a `stream.print` call without keywords needs no text schema at all.
- [IO-4] `stream.print` and `stream.write` contribute the `output` effect. `noio` withholds `filesystem`, `network` and `output` together, so a direct or transitive `stream.print` under `noio` is rejected with `E_EFFECT_BOUND` and a call witness; `effects(output)` permits it and `noalloc` does not constrain it. Bytecode validation independently recomputes the effect from the emitted instruction.
- [IO-5] `stream.print` is checked only on a receiver whose type is `Stream`; a bare `print`/`eprint` reports `E_OUTPUT_CAPABILITY` with the fix to add `out: Stream` and pass one from main, while a local binding or declared function named `print` remains an ordinary name. Capabilities, files, owned resources and callback values have no rendering and are rejected with `E_IO_ARGUMENT`. A host that supplies no output writer discards program output rather than failing, and a writer that refuses bytes traps with `R_OUTPUT`.
- [IO-6] The QBE native backend lowers `stream.print`. The compiler emits one C renderer per type a program actually prints, so every value shape renders byte-identically to `src/io_render.zig`, including `sep=`/`end=` raw text, the 24-level depth cap and the float spelling. A call buffers its whole line before any byte reaches the stream and flushes once; a refused write traps `R_OUTPUT`. Generic function bodies reject `stream.print` and the whole-path file builtins with `E_GENERIC_BODY_UNSUPPORTED` rather than reporting them as unknown functions.
- [IO-7] `with` is one statement form with two resource kinds. `with arena() as name:` opens a managed region; any other expression must produce an open file handle and must name its binding. `open(fs, path, mode)` takes a `Filesystem` capability, a relative `str` path and a literal mode — `"r"`, `"w"`, `"a"` with an optional `"b"` or `"t"` — and returns `Result[File, str]` for binary modes or `Result[TextFile, str]` for text modes. Directly as the head of a `with` statement the call yields the handle itself and a failure traps with a located `R_FILE`, the way Python's `open` raises; `with open(...)? as f:` propagates the result instead, and every other position yields the ordinary result. A binary handle's `read`, `readline` and `readlines` produce `bytes`; a text handle's produce `str`, decoded as UTF-8. `f.read()` reads to end of file, `f.read(n)` reads at most `n` bytes, `f.readline()` keeps its terminator, and `f.readlines()` returns the lines with their terminators kept.
- [IO-8] `f.write(data)` accepts the handle's own element type, writes every byte by looping over the provider's partial progress, and returns the count. `f.flush()` exists for familiarity and is a no-op because writes are unbuffered. Mode `"w"` creates the file or truncates an existing one, `"a"` appends at the current end, and `"r"` neither creates nor truncates. Whole-file reads and writes are capped at 64 MiB.
- [IO-9] `read_file`, `read_text`, `write_file`, `write_text`, `listdir`, `mkdir` and `remove` each take the capability first and return `Result[T, str]`, whose error text names the operation, the path and a readable reason. `exists(fs, path)` returns `bool` rather than a result. A helper opens, uses and closes its resource within the call, so a failure leaves no handle behind.
- [IO-10] A directory granted without write authority rejects every writing mode — `w`, `a`, create, truncate, `mkdir` and `remove` — before opening anything, and the returned error says the mode needs a write grant.
- [IO-11] A file `with` scope closes its handle exactly once at every exit: falling off the end, `return`, `?` propagation, `break`, `continue`, an explicit `f.close()`, and a trap or host allocation failure unwinding through it. A method on a closed or stale handle traps with `R_FILE`; so does any provider failure during a method, since `f.read()` yields bytes rather than a result. The trap message carries the same readable reason as the `Result` error text.
- [IO-12] File operations contribute the `filesystem` effect, so `noio` rejects them with `E_EFFECT_BOUND`. `with` on a value that is not an arena or an open file is rejected with `E_WITH_RESOURCE`, which tells a `Result` operand to propagate with `open(...)?` or match first. A non-literal mode, an unsupported mode and an unknown handle method are rejected with `E_FILE_ARGUMENT`. Generic function bodies reject file operations. The QBE native backend runs the whole file surface too, over a native scoped-directory provider with the same request grammar, `Result` error text and located `R_FILE` traps; an open handle is one owned resource word that the ordinary local drop closes at every scope exit. A native executable grants every capability by default and `--restrict` switches it to deny-by-default with the same `--allow-*` flags, which `mylang build --run` forwards after `--`.
- [IO-13] A `class` value renders its own fields wherever it is nested — a union or option payload, a record or class field, a tuple or fixed-array element, a list or dictionary element — exactly as it renders at the top level. Every generated renderer receives the address of its value's slot, and a class slot holds the body pointer, so the class renderer loads that pointer before reading any field; no renderer reads a field at the slot itself.
- [IO-14] `stream.print` renders every finite `float` in its whole positional spelling, however long: the renderer's frame holds the longest finite double (the smallest subnormal's `0.` and 323 zeros, or the largest double's 309 integer digits), so `1.2345e300`, `-1.7976931348623157e308` and `5e-324` print digit for digit as `str(x)` spells them, where a value longer than 64 characters used to leave the line unprintable and trap `InternalError`.
- [IO-15] `print` and `write` are methods of any `Stream`-typed receiver, not only of a bare name: a `Stream` held in a class or record field prints and writes through the field expression (`sink.stream.write(data)`, `pair.first.stream.print(...)`) exactly as a bound local does, with the same `output` effect, return value and lowering. Such a receiver takes positional arguments only (`sep=`/`end=` still need the stream bound to a name), and any other method on it is `E_COLLECTION_METHOD` with a message naming `print` and `write` instead of the collection methods.
- [REQUIRED-PROJECT-IMPORT-POLICY-1] A `mylang.json` discovered by walking up from the entry source file to the filesystem root makes its `import_policy` required for ordinary `check`, `check-project`, `build`, `run`, `run-project`, project variants and native test preparation, evaluated before any cached success and never selected from the shell working directory. Missing or malformed configuration is a host failure (status 2); a policy violation is status 3 and language or import-cycle errors stay status 1. Policy and configuration identity participates in verdict caches and receipts so a stricter policy invalidates an earlier pass, import-cycle checking cannot be disabled, and projects without a configuration keep their prior behavior.
- [TESTS-FOR-1] `mylang tests-for <entry.my> <qualified-name>` loads and checks the project, then prints the discovered tests that reach that declaration: a `direct` list of tests whose body calls it directly, and a `reached-by` list of every test that reaches it transitively. It is a selection aid, not a verdict: an unknown qualified name prints a message and exits 2, and a declaration no test reaches prints empty lists and exits 0.
- [REF-DIFF-1] The commit-time rules identify changed declarations from `git diff --unified=0 <reference>` run in the entry file's own repository: the changed working-tree line ranges come from the `+` side of each hunk header, and a declaration is changed when its source line range intersects one. A failed git invocation is a host failure, and the reference revision is never recompiled.
- [CHANGE-WITHOUT-TEST-1] Under `mylang lint --gate --against REF` only, every non-test function whose declaration changed since `REF` must be the direct callee of a test whose own body changed or was added in the same diff, be exempt under `test-coverage.exempt`, or carry a `change-without-test` waiver; otherwise it reports `L_CHANGE_WITHOUT_TEST` at error severity with a related note naming the tests that currently call it directly. A plain `lint --gate` without `--against`, and a baseline row under `.mylang/lint/`, never suppress it, because the rule judges a diff rather than the standing tree.
- [API-DIFF-1] `mylang api-diff <entry.my> --write=FILE` records an `api-diff-baseline-v1` snapshot of every declaration's stable identity and its interface and body fingerprints; `--against=FILE` classifies the current project against one snapshot as `additive` (a new identity), `compatible` (only the body fingerprint changed), `breaking` (the identity disappeared or its interface fingerprint changed) and `unchanged`, printing a one-line summary and the changed declarations by default or one `api-diff-v1` JSON object with `--json`. It is a report and never blocks: exactly one of `--write`/`--against` is required, and a malformed or unreadable baseline exits 2.
- [LINT-2] A `mylang.json` version 2 may carry a `lint` object: `profile` (`agent` default, `strict`, `safety`), `on_build` (`report` default, `block`), `warnings` (`report` default, `deny`), positive integer `min_chars` (the `undocumented-export` docstring floor, 12 by default), `block`/`ratchet`/`report` arrays of rule names, and a `rules` object whose per-rule settings carry positive integer `max`, `hard_max` and `recover_pct`, reasoned `exempt` and `allow` entries, and `modules` globs. Unknown or duplicate keys, malformed values, a non-object `lint`, and an exemption without a name or a non-empty reason are located configuration failures (status 2); `lint` requires `version: 2`, and a version 1 document keeps parsing without it. A configured `lint` object participates in verdict cache identity exactly as `import_policy` does.
- [LINT-1] `mylang lint <entry.my|dir>` loads the project through the shared frontend, runs every enabled rule over the checked program, prints each finding as an `L_*` diagnostic on stderr and one summary line on stdout. It exits 0 when the language is valid and no finding blocks, 1 for a source, type, effect or ownership error with no rule evaluated, 2 for a usage or configuration failure, and 3 when a blocking finding exists. Without `--gate` a `block` rule is reported at warning severity and never changes the exit unless the project sets `on_build: "block"`; `--gate` raises each block finding to error severity and refuses. `--json` (or `--diagnostics=json`) renders findings as `diagnostic-v1` JSON Lines on stderr, and `--profile=agent|strict|safety` overrides the configured profile for one run. A `# lint: allow <rule> reason="..."` comment on the line above a declaration waives that rule for it; a waiver without a reason is `L_WAIVER_REASON` and always blocks. The rules are `untested-export` (report under `agent`, block under `strict` and `safety`), `undocumented-export` (report under `agent`, block under `strict` and `safety`; a missing, too-short or name-restating docstring on an exported function, record, union or domain), `dead-private` (a private function with no call site), and the test-hygiene rules `test-no-assertion` (a test body whose every `return` is the literal `true` and which never asserts), `test-conditional` (a branch or match, or more than one loop, at a test body top level) and `test-empty` (an empty test body), all blocking under every profile. The advisory size and reach report-only rules — `function-length`, `parameters`, `nesting-depth`, `decision-points` and `dead-export` — are `.warning` under every profile and can never refuse; their thresholds come from `lint.rules.<name>.max` and default to 80 lines, 6 parameters, nesting 4, 10 decision points, and no caller anywhere in the project respectively.
- [LINT-3] `mylang lint --accept` writes the baseline and ratchet stores under `.mylang/lint/`, keyed on the checker's resolved declaration identity rather than message text. A baseline row `<module>|<qualified>` marks one existing finding as accepted debt: under `--gate` a blocking finding with a row is reported at warning severity and does not refuse, while a finding without a row refuses. A ratchet row `<qualified> <ceiling>` records a per-declaration ceiling for a threshold rule that may only shrink. A run without `--accept` never writes, prunes or lowers a store, baseline rows are sorted so the committed file is merge-friendly, and a store file containing merge conflict markers is refused rather than merged.
- [LINT-4] `integration-tests` (`L_TEST_CAPABILITY`) reports a discovered test whose declared parameters request `Filesystem`, `Network`, `Clock` or `Entropy` and whose module does not match the rule's `modules` globs (default `*_integration`, `integration.*`), because such a test is an integration test and belongs in an integration module. It reports under every profile and never blocks; a reasoned `exempt` entry or a matching module glob leaves the test alone.
- [LINT-5] The tests-reachability index built for `mylang tests-for` is shared: every `mylang lint` finding that names a declaration carries the discovered tests that reach it as a related note (a rule that set its own related note keeps it, and a declaration no test reaches gets none), and `mylang list-interfaces` prints a `tests(N)` column on each exported callable when bodies were checked, where `N` is the number of discovered tests that reach it directly or transitively.
- [LINT-6] The advisory section-9 report rules ship under every profile and can never block: `record-size` (`L_RECORD_SIZE`, a record or class over `rules.record-size.max`, default 7 fields), `file-length` (`L_FILE_LENGTH`, a module over `rules.file-length.max`, default 1000 non-blank non-comment lines), `bool-ops-per-condition` (`L_BOOL_OPS`, a single branch or loop condition combining more than `rules.bool-ops-per-condition.max` `and`/`or` operators, default 3), `naming` (`L_NAMING`, a function, parameter, local, record, union or domain that is not snake_case, PascalCase or off the vague-name list), `repeated-literal` (`L_REPEATED_LITERAL`, an integer literal other than `0`/`1` appearing at least three times in one module), `same-type-adjacent-params` (`L_SAME_TYPE_ADJACENT_PARAMS`, adjacent parameters sharing a non-capability type), `duplicate-body` (`L_DUPLICATE_BODY`, two or more checked functions with an identical normalized body, never blocking) and `output-in-library` (`L_OUTPUT_IN_LIBRARY`, a function whose inferred effects include `output` in a module outside `rules.output-in-library.modules`).

## Collection and statement surface

- [SURFACE-COLLECTIONS-1] Managed list push(value), len(), indexing, and indexed assignment preserve shared aliases across bindings.
- [SURFACE-COLLECTIONS-2] Record construction names its type and fields — `Box[i64](value=42)`, `Empty()` — covering generic records, empty records declared with `pass`, and trailing argument commas.
- [SURFACE-COLLECTIONS-3] Generic managed list methods and indexing preserve abstract element type checking.

- [SURFACE-STMT-7] Condition expressions admit record constructors inside nested call arguments; record and field names remain case-sensitive identifiers.
- [SURFACE-STMT-8] Record constructor syntax accepts nested generic type arguments, including List fields initialized with contextually typed empty lists.
- [SURFACE-STMT-9] A newline separates postfix call suffixes from completed expressions outside parentheses, while parentheses permit multiline calls and suffixes.

- [SURFACE-COLLECTIONS-4] Managed list methods reject invalid argument counts, unknown methods, mismatched element types, and noninteger indices.

## Prelude types and contextual constructors

- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-1] List<T> and Result<T,E> are available without imports or explicit declarations and use existing managed-list and fallible-result semantics.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-2] Empty lists and Ok/Err payload constructors use expected binding, return, and concrete call-argument types.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-3] Record fields and managed list elements propagate their expected types into nested empty list expressions.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-4] Ok(()) constructs successful Result<unit,E> values with normal handling obligations.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-5] Generic List and Result expressions preserve symbolic type parameters through empty list creation, mutation, and Ok construction.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-6] Contextual constructors reject missing or incompatible expected types, invalid generic arity, and forbidden managed capability elements.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-7] Explicitly declared List and Result types retain their own nominal identities and contextual constructors.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-8] Builtin List and Result types share canonical identity across imported module signatures without imports of a prelude module.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-9] A module-local List declaration shadows the prelude in that module while unrelated modules continue to resolve the builtin List.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-10] Module-local functions named Ok or Err take precedence over contextual constructors in both ordinary and generic bodies, without spurious constructor diagnostics.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-11] A list method's operands are expected types too: `append`, `remove` and `index` expect the element type, `insert` an integer position and the element type, and `extend` the list type, so `rows.append([])` infers the empty literal as `rows: list[list[int]] = []` does and `octets.append(200)` on a `list[u8]` types the literal as `u8`; `bytes([])` and `byte_buffer([])` infer `list[int]`.
- [SCHEMATIC-INPUTS-1] The schematic host provides named circuit, pinout, and interface documents through io.inputs; typed Document values carry the document identity and an owned immutable byte copy loaded through bounded host blocks. Older guests declaring only the original scalar byte callbacks remain supported by the provider.
- [SCHEMATIC-INPUTS-2] Named input providers reject absent host documents instead of issuing unusable handles.
- [HOST-GUEST-POLICY-1] A host application names the code generator and the accounting its guest is built with, and runs that guest under exactly the ceilings the build can enforce. `.qbe` passes `--qbe PATH`, which pins the assembler; `.llvm` passes `--backend llvm` and pins none, so the driver resolves the release code generator and its adapter. `.steps` accounting passes `--step-accounting` and bounds one invocation with `--max-steps=`; `.frames` accounting passes `--max-frames=N` instead and bounds one invocation with that same call-depth ceiling and a `--timeout=` wall clock, counting no instructions. The invocation's ceilings are derived from the policy rather than restated by the caller, so a guest is never handed a ceiling whose accounting its build dropped, and a caller's own tighter ceiling is the one the runtime reads first.
- [SCHEMATIC-RUNNER-POLICY-1] `schematic-svg`, its `--dev-session` and its whole-fixture test build the guest the way a release `mylang build` does — the LLVM code generator at `-O3`, no instruction accounting — instead of pinning the debug assembler and counting every instruction. `--backend qbe` selects QBE and drops the release path's LLVM controls, `--llvm-opt`, `--llvm-adapter` and `--lto` override the rest, and `--bounded` restores instruction accounting and its `--max-steps=` ceiling.
- [SCHEMATIC-RUNNER-BOUNDS-1] A runaway guest under the shipped policy is still stopped: unbounded recursion trapping at the call-depth ceiling, a loop that never leaves trapping at the wall clock, and a runaway allocation trapping at the byte ceiling. None of the three publishes SVG.

- [MODULE-DOT-1] Direct import aliases accept dot-qualified function and type references, including generic records and imported domain methods; local list methods retain their meanings.
- [MODULE-DOT-2] Dot-qualified imported functions can be passed as values, and generic function bodies resolve dotted module type references through their declaring module.
- [MODULE-DOT-3] Dotted qualification preserves private-symbol, private-domain-function, and import-alias-shadowing checks.
- [MODULE-DOT-4] Dotted module parsing, namespace normalization, checking, and execution release all allocations on allocation failure.

- [SCHEMATIC-MODEL-1] The schematic entry parses named document handles, builds one validated schematic value, and passes it to rendering; normal and collection-stress execution produce identical SVG and release all managed roots.
- [MODULE-DOT-5] Semantic caching recognizes dotted imported function values and keeps callback type catalogs fresh across repeated requests and imported function edits.
- [MODULE-DOT-6] An import admits the full dotted module path (`a.b.name`) and its local alias — the explicit `as` name, or the trailing segment (`b.name`) when there is none — as qualified function and type references. A bare prefix that does not continue a registered module path (`shared` when `shared.geometry` is imported) is not reserved, so a local or parameter may reuse it; only a segment that continues the path is joined. A multi-segment qualified name normalizes by splitting at its last dot.

## Python-oriented core values

- [PYTHON-CORE-1] Prelude List, Result and Option have one nominal identity shared across modules; unrelated user nominal declarations remain distinct.
- [PYTHON-CORE-2] Unit and Result<unit/int,int> entry functions adapt to the scalar runner status convention while retaining their ordinary guest signatures. The synthesized wrapper is not a source declaration, so no public interface names it and a project with such an entry emits native units like any other.
- [PYTHON-CORE-3] Representable unsuffixed integer constants inherit numeric contexts; explicit suffixes retain their types and out-of-range constants are rejected.
- [PYTHON-CORE-4] Decimal float literals and ordinary arithmetic/comparisons use binary64 numeric semantics, including equal signed zeros and unordered NaNs.

## Surface syntax

- [PYTHON-1] Sources use `def`/`record`/`class` heads, colon and space-indented blocks, `#` comments, implicit continuation inside delimiters, and source-preserving virtual block delimiters. A suite opens at the `:` that ends its line and closes at its dedent; `{` and `}` are only a dictionary literal, so a brace-bodied declaration is rejected. A suite's reported bytes end at the last token it wrote, so the blank lines and comments before the next declaration belong to neither, and a function's measured length and navigation excerpt cover only the function. Missing blocks, inconsistent dedents, and indentation tabs are diagnosed. Indentation is the only block syntax, so `fmt` returns the text a program already has.
- [PYTHON-2] Functions support ordinary mutable assignment, annotated bindings, `True`/`False`/`None`, `and`/`or`/`not`, `pass`, `elif`, omitted unit return annotations, `int` as i64, and square type parameters whose omitted requirement defaults to copy. `pass` spells an empty suite wherever one is written, including a record with no fields and a domain with no members. A `;` separates two statements written on one line, as it does in Python.
- [ATTR-1] A declaration may carry a fixed-vocabulary attribute on the line above it: @deprecated("reason"), @test, or @allow("rule", reason="…"). The parser rejects any other @name with E_UNKNOWN_ATTRIBUTE and a repeated attribute on one declaration with E_DUPLICATE_ATTRIBUTE. Attributes are part of the declaration header and the formatter round-trips them.
- [ATTR-2] @test makes a zero-parameter bool function a discovered test regardless of its name or export flag; mylang test, the native test schema and the W2 test rules agree on that set, and @test is reported in list-declarations and list-interfaces --contracts.
- [ATTR-3] A call to a @deprecated declaration from another module reports W_DEPRECATED naming the reason; the attribute participates in the declaration and project interface fingerprints, so removing a declaration deprecated in the baseline is an interface-compatible change once api-diff exists.
- [ATTR-4] @allow("rule", reason="…") on a declaration suppresses that lint rule for findings on the declaration and replaces the # lint: allow comment form; a reason is still mandatory (L_WAIVER_REASON). The comment form is still accepted alongside the attribute this round.
- [DOC-1] A triple- or single-quoted string literal that is the first statement of a module or of a function body is that declaration's docstring; it is inert, contributes no effect, and later standalone strings stay ordinary discarded expressions. list-interfaces shows its first line.
- [DOC-2] L_UNDOCUMENTED_EXPORT reports an exported function, record, union or domain with no docstring, one shorter than min_chars (12 by default, counted over non-whitespace characters), or one that merely restates the declaration name. It reports under agent and blocks under strict.
- [PY-RECORD-1] `record Name:` is the canonical declaration head for a nominal value record with annotated fields and named construction. `record` remains an ordinary identifier outside declaration position, so variables and parameters may still be named `record`; `class Name:` remains accepted as the compatibility spelling during the migration window.
- [PYTHON-3] Value records use `record` (or the `class` compatibility spelling) with annotated fields and named construction; square-bracket type applications and `match`/`case` preserve contextual variant names and underscore payload discards for semantic resolution. Every explicit type argument uses square brackets, including `todo[T]()`, `wrap[D](v)`, `codec_encode[D](v)` and the capability and resource operations.
- [PY-LAMBDA-1] `lambda name, ...: expression` is an expression-bodied spelling for the existing noncapturing function value. Its parameter and result types come from the expected `def(...) -> ... effects(...)` callback type, and invocation uses the same checked callback path as a named function value.
- [PY-LAMBDA-2] A lambda without an expected callback type is rejected. Its expression must match the expected result and exact effect ceiling; it may read its parameters and module constants but cannot capture a function local, whose diagnostic recommends a top-level function or explicit state parameter.

- [PY-VARIANT-1] `Type.case(value)` constructs an enum or tagged-union variant carrying its declared payload; `Type.case` without parentheses stays the payload-free construction and contextual `Ok`/`Err`/`Some`/`None` are unchanged. The payload evaluates once and `match`/`case` patterns keep the same spelling.
- [PY-GENERIC-1] `f[T](x)` applies explicit type arguments to a generic function, constant lengths included (`first[i64, 2](values)`), and a member call takes them the same way: `D.hidden[U](x)` on a domain method, `m.D.make[U](x)` on an imported one, and `m.make[i64, 2](v)` on an imported free function. An argument may name any type the lexer spells as a keyword, capabilities included: `hold[clock_cap]()`. A square-bracket application in call position names a function when the base is a declared function and a generic record otherwise, so `Box[T](field=v)` construction is unchanged and the nominal type catalog never resolves a function application as a type.
- [PY-GENERIC-2] Explicit call type arguments reach the same instance as inference, and the wrong number of arguments or an undeclared base name is rejected.
- [PY-FLOAT-1] `sqrt`, `floor`, `ceil`, `trunc` and `abs` are prelude operations on `float`, so guest code needs no `u64` bit pattern. Each takes one float and answers a float; a non-float argument is rejected, and a local binding or declared function of the same name shadows the operation.
- [PY-WILDCARD-1] `case _` in a `match` statement covers every variant no earlier arm named, discarding the payload of each one it covers.
- [PY-WILDCARD-2] A wildcard arm must be the last arm, must cover at least one variant, and is rejected when it would drop a result-bearing payload; that variant needs its own arm.
- [PY-SUBSCRIPT-1] A subscript index may begin with a parenthesis: `values[(n - 1) % 3]` is an index, because a square-bracket type application never contains a parenthesized group.
- [PY-RESERVED-1] `len` and `replace` are intrinsics only at the head of a call. Both name ordinary declarations, record fields, keyword arguments, members, exports and imported module members, so `strings.replace(...)`, `pool.len(p)`, `text.replace(old, new)` and a field named `len` are all spellable.
- [PY-LOOP-1] A `while True` loop with no `break` targeting it never falls through, so a function whose body ends in one needs no unreachable trailing return.
- [PY-PRELUDE-1] The `bytes` and `float` prelude types are registered wherever their names are written in source, including a variant payload, a record field and a bare annotation, rather than only where a literal or conversion appears.
- [PRELUDE-FOLD-1] `sum(xs)` adds every element of a `list[int]` or `list[float]` in order, starting from zero of the element type; an empty list sums to zero and any other element type is rejected.
- [PRELUDE-FOLD-2] `any(xs)` and `all(xs)` fold a `list[bool]` with `or` and `and`, stopping as soon as `any` sees `True` or `all` sees `False`; an empty list answers `False` and `True` respectively.
- [PRELUDE-EXTREME-1] `min` and `max` take a list of `int`, `float` or `str` values, or two scalars of one type, and answer an element. Text order compares the stored Unicode scalars directly and contributes no managed allocation, so a text extrema fold is accepted under `noalloc`. An empty list has no smallest element, so it traps `R_LIST_BOUNDS` at the call site.
- [PRELUDE-SORT-1] `sorted(xs)` returns a new ascending list of `int`, `float` or `str` elements, leaving the input list and every alias of it unchanged. It uses the same stable `O(n log n)` native merge engine as in-place sorting with one reusable work buffer. Equal elements and signed zeros keep their input order, NaNs follow every ordered float, and `str` values order by Unicode code point, matching `sort.compare_strs`.
- [PRELUDE-SORT-KEY-1] `sorted(xs, key=callback, reverse=flag)` copies the list and sorts the copy exactly as `values.sort(key=callback, reverse=flag)` sorts in place (LIST-SORT-KEY-1): any ordered key, each computed once in input order, stable with and without `reverse`. The input remains unchanged, the list is positional, and `key` and `reverse` are its only named arguments, both optional and keyword-only.
- [PRELUDE-ABS-1] `abs` is total for `int`: it answers the magnitude and traps `R_OVERFLOW` on the one value with no positive counterpart, `-9223372036854775808`. A `float` argument still lowers to the prelude float operation.
- [PRELUDE-ROUND-1] `round(value)` takes a `float` and answers the nearest integral `float`, with halves rounded away from zero, matching `math.round`. Not-a-number and the infinities pass through unchanged.
- [PRELUDE-ITERATION-1] `reversed(xs)` answers a new list of a list or string `xs` from last to first and is also a `for` source; `zip(a, b)` is a `for` source that walks two lists in step and stops at the shorter one; `list(...)` copies a list and materialises `list(range(end))` or `list(range(start, end))`.
- [PRELUDE-SHADOW-1] Every prelude builtin answers only when no local binding, declared function or module top-level name claims it, the same shadowing rule the other prelude operations follow.

- [PY-COMPREHENSION-1] `[value for name in iterable if condition]` builds a fresh list through one checked loop over a list, string, byte string or dictionary, under the same fixed iteration bound and mutation check as `for`. An unfiltered comprehension reserves that bound before filling the list; a filtered comprehension retains ordinary growth so a sparse result does not reserve the full source size. The filter runs before the value, the binding lives only inside the comprehension, and the source collection is unchanged.
- [PY-COMPREHENSION-2] A comprehension takes exactly one generator and at most one filter. A binding that shadows an active name, a non-Boolean filter, a source that is not a managed collection or an integer range, and a produced value that is not a copyable storable list element are all rejected. Generator expressions and multiple generators are not provided.
- [PY-COMPREHENSION-3] A comprehension accepts `range(...)` as its source exactly as a `for` loop does: `range(end)` and `range(start, end)` iterate a half-open ascending integer range whose bounds share one integer type, the binding takes each integer value, and the produced list has `int` elements when the body is the binding itself. The bounds evaluate once before the loop and an empty or reversed range yields an empty list. An unfiltered range comprehension and `list(range(...))` reserve the exact nonnegative range length with overflow-safe signed and unsigned arithmetic.
- [PY-FSTRING-1] `f"..."` interpolates `{expression}` holes and constructs the pieces into one `str` with one exactly-sized result allocation rather than a chain of growing-prefix concatenations. A single piece returns its rendered text directly without a join or intermediate list. Holes evaluate and render exactly once from left to right. A hole takes any value `print` can render — lists, dictionaries, records, variants, results, options and byte strings as well as scalars — and renders it through the same renderer, so one value has one spelling wherever it is written. A hole holds one ordinary expression, including calls, operators, indexing and a nested format string, and is checked in its own source position. `f` before a quote is the prefix; anywhere else it is an ordinary identifier.
- [PY-FSTRING-2] `{{` and `}}` stand for single braces, ordinary string escapes apply to the literal pieces, and an empty format string is empty text. An empty hole, an unmatched or unterminated brace, more than one expression in a hole, a format specification after `:`, and a value with no rendering — a capability, an open file or a callback — are all rejected. Rendering releases its partial buffer on allocation failure and reports it as `OutOfMemory`, the way every other allocating writer in the compiler does.
- [PY-SCOPE-1] Locals are function-scoped, like Python: an assignment to a name anywhere in a function body refers to one binding that is in scope from its first assignment to the end of the body, and a nested block reassigns the same binding rather than introducing a new one. The binding's type is fixed at its first assignment and later assignments stay assignment-compatible. A `for` loop variable and a `match` payload binding remain scoped to their own block, and a move-only resource stays confined to the block that must discharge its cleanup.
- [PY-FOR-REBIND-1] A `for` loop variable is rebound from the loop at the top of every iteration, exactly as in Python, so assigning it in the body — `i = 10`, `i += 3`, a destructuring target `i, x = pair` — changes the variable for the rest of that iteration and never which iteration comes next or how many run. This holds for every loop form: `range(end)` and `range(start, end)`, both names of `enumerate`, the element of a list, string, bytes, dictionary, dictionary view or fixed array, `reversed`, both names of `zip` and of a two-value tuple or dictionary items, nested loops, and the same forms in a generic function body or a compile-time function's `range` loop. A counting loop — `range` or an `enumerate` position — whose body never assigns its variable counts on that variable itself, which is then immutable, so its lowering is unchanged and `PY-ARRAY-NEGATIVE-1` may treat it as never negative; a body that can assign it (read from the syntax before the body is checked: an assignment, annotated binding or destructuring target naming it, or a place write rooted at it) gets its own mutable variable, copied from a hidden counter at the top of each iteration and not treated as non-negative. `continue` still advances the hidden counter; `break` still skips it.
- [PY-SCOPE-2] Reading a function-scoped local that no control-flow path reaching the read has assigned is rejected with `E_UNBOUND` naming where the binding is first assigned; a name never assigned before the read is still `E_UNKNOWN_NAME`. An assignment in every continuing branch of an `if`/`elif`/`else` binds the name afterwards, and an assignment in only some branches does not. Every `while`, ordinary `for`, `range`, `reversed` and `zip` loop may execute zero times, so assignments made only in the loop body do not bind the name after the loop; a name read after the loop must have been predeclared before it.
- [PY-SCOPE-3] Every `match` arm starts from the same pre-match definite-assignment state. A local is definitely assigned after the match only when every continuing arm assigned it; terminating arms contribute no continuation. A wildcard body counts as the continuation path for every variant it covers. Payload bindings remain scoped to their arm, while an ordinary local introduced in an arm remains function-scoped and must be assigned on every continuing path before it can be read after the match.
- [PY-STR-1] `str` values answer `strip`, `lstrip`, `rstrip`, `upper`, `lower` and `zfill(width)` with a fresh string, leaving the receiver and every alias unchanged. Case mapping covers ASCII letters and leaves every other scalar alone; `zfill` keeps a leading sign in front of the padding.
- [PY-STR-2] `replace(old, new)`, `find(sub)` (-1 when absent), `count(sub)`, `startswith`, `endswith`, `isdigit`, `isalpha` and `isspace` follow Python's answers, including `False` for every classification of empty text. An empty needle traps `R_EMPTY_SEPARATOR`, and these methods are rejected on lists and dictionaries.
- [PY-STR-3] `splitlines()` splits on `\n`, `\r` and `\r\n` without keeping the terminators and yields no line for empty text. `int(text)` returns `Option[int]` and `float(text)` returns `Option[float]`, answering `None` rather than raising when the whole string is not exactly one number.
- [PY-STR-4] Every `str` method, scalar-value iteration and both number intrinsics lower to the QBE native backend over the immutable native text runtime, so no `str` operation reports `E_QBE_UNSUPPORTED`. A native `find`, `count` or `replace` with an empty needle raises `R_EMPTY_SEPARATOR`, and every method answers the value its documented semantics require.
- [PY-NUM-1] `int(text)` and `float(text)` accept one pinned base-10 grammar: optional surrounding ASCII whitespace, an optional `+` or `-` sign, then decimal ASCII digits separated by single `_` characters that must sit between two digits. `float` additionally accepts a `.` fraction, an `e`/`E` exponent with its own optional sign and digit run, and the case-insensitive spellings `inf`, `infinity` and `nan`. An alternate base such as `0x10`, a non-ASCII digit, an interior space, a trailing character and empty text are all `None`, and one shared vector table drives the checker and the native runtime so they cannot drift.
- [PY-NUM-3] A native program that parses numbers links the shared text runtime unit that holds the grammar, and `int(text)`/`float(text)` lower to an `Option` whose `Some` payload the runtime publishes and whose `None` carries no payload. A program that only measures or slices text links no such unit.
- [PY-NUM-2] `int(text)` answers `None` for a value the signed 64-bit range cannot hold rather than wrapping. `float(text)` is correctly rounded: a value beyond binary64 answers an infinity and one below it a signed zero, an absurd exponent saturates instead of wrapping, and digits past the rounding horizon are folded into a sticky digit that keeps the rounding the full text would have produced.
- [PY-ASSERT-1] `assert condition` checks a Boolean expression under any effect bound, in ordinary and generic function bodies, and traps `R_ASSERT` when it is false. The trap's source span is the checked expression and its default message is that expression's source text.
- [PY-ASSERT-2] `assert condition, "message"` reports the string literal instead of the expression text. Native lowering raises the same `R_ASSERT` code without the message, matching every other native trap.
- [PY-ASSERT-3] The message after assert may be any str expression, including an f-string; it is evaluated only when the condition is false and rendered to standard error immediately before the `R_ASSERT` trap, so a passing assertion pays nothing and a failed one names the values it saw. Because that expression may allocate, its effects count against the enclosing function's declared ceiling: a literal message stays free of effects, and an allocating message under `noalloc` or `effects()` is rejected.
- [PY-DIV-1] `divtrunc(a, b)` and `remtrunc(a, b)` are prelude helpers for integer division and remainder that round toward zero, the rounding `//` and `%` deliberately do not provide. They take two operands of one integer type, trap on a zero divisor and on the signed minimum divided by -1, and a local binding or declared function of the same name shadows them.
- [PY-CONVERSION-1] A `conversion` function body constructs its destination record as `T(a=v)`, under complete source-field accounting.
- [PY-VARIANT-3] A generic variant names only its template, because `Slot[T].some(v)` cannot be told apart from an index followed by a call. `Slot.some(v)` and the pattern `case Slot.some(v)` take their type arguments from the expected type — a binding annotation, parameter, field or result — in ordinary and generic function bodies alike, and report `E_GENERIC_INFERENCE` when no context supplies them. A project resolves the template through its own module names, so a declared or imported `m.Slot.some(v)` infers its arguments the same way.
- [PY-ARRAY-1] A list literal whose expected type is a fixed array `[N]T` — from an annotation, a parameter, a record field or a return type — constructs that array rather than a managed list, and a list literal in every other context still builds a managed list.
- [PY-ARRAY-2] Fixed-array literals check their length statically against the expected array, reporting `E_ARRAY_LENGTH`, and each element against the declared element type.
- [PY-ARRAY-WRITE-1] A fixed array `[N]T` or enum table `[Kind]T` is written in place with the list spelling: `a[i] = v`, `a[i] op= v`, `a[i].f = v`, `a[i].f.g = v`, `a[i][j] = v` for an array of arrays, `r.cells[i] = v` through a value record, `c.cells[i] = v` through a `class`, and `xs[k][j] = v` or `xs[k].cells[j] = v` below a list or dictionary slot. The write is `a = replace(a, i, v)` for each array step and a record update for each field step, stored back into the binding the path starts at — or into the last list or dictionary slot on the path, which is a reference — so value semantics are those of `replace`: `b = a` copies, and a copy taken before the write keeps its value. The binding must be mutable (`E_IMMUTABLE` otherwise) and copyable. The assigned value is evaluated before the target's indices, an index is evaluated once even though it both reads and rebuilds the path, and a value that could write the binding (a call) is computed before the binding is read, so nothing it writes is lost. An index follows the fixed-array index rule of `PY-ARRAY-NEGATIVE-1` exactly as a read and `replace` do: a negative index counts from the end, and an index outside `-N <= i < N` traps `R_LIST_BOUNDS` with the index as written and the length at the write's source position. A write whose path ends inside a `str` or `bytes`, or that is rooted anywhere other than a binding or a list or dictionary slot, keeps its existing diagnostic, and inside `with arena()` a managed value stored into an array declared before the block is `E_ARENA_ESCAPE` as `outer[index] = value` is for a list.
- [PY-ARRAY-WRITE-2] Augmented assignment to a field below an array element, `a[i].f op= v` (and `r.cells[i].f op= v`, `xs[k][j].f op= v`), keeps its operator: the element's field is read once through the once-evaluated path, the operand is evaluated, the pair is combined with the operator's ordinary checked rules and the result is written back exactly as `a[i].f = combined` is, the way `record.field op= v` and `a[i] op= v` already are.
- [PY-ARRAY-NEGATIVE-1] A fixed array `[N]T` takes Python's negative indices exactly as a list does: `a[-1]` is the last element and `a[-N]` the first, for a read, for every element write of `PY-ARRAY-WRITE-1` (`a[i] = v`, `a[i] op= v`, `a[i].f = v`, `a[i][j] = v`, a path below a record, `class` or list slot) and for `replace(a, i, v)`. An index outside `-N <= i < N` traps `R_LIST_BOUNDS` with the index as the program wrote it and the length, the code and detail a list reports; an index is still evaluated once. An enum table `[Kind]T` is indexed by its key and has no negative positions. The rule costs nothing where the checker can see the index is never negative — a non-negative constant, an unsigned value, a length, the induction variable of a `for` or comprehension over `range` from a non-negative start, an `enumerate` position the loop body never assigns, and a checked `+`, `*`, `//` or truncating remainder of such values, a `%` by a positive constant, a `&` with one, a right shift of one — and a constant in `-N .. -1` is folded to its position from the start; any other signed index is handled without a branch: one unsigned test of `i + N` against `2N` replaces the ordinary bounds test, and the element address is the ordinary `base + i * stride` plus `N * stride` selected by the sign of `i`.
- [PY-ARRAY-ITER-1] `for x in a`, `for i, x in enumerate(a)`, a two-name loop over an array of two-value tuples and `[f(x) for x in a]` walk a fixed array `[N]T` from index 0 to `N - 1`. The loop reads the value the array had when the loop began, as a list loop reads its initial length: element writes in the body change the array but not the elements the loop visits, and no concurrent-modification guard is needed because a fixed array cannot change length. An enum table `[Kind]T` is not walked by position and keeps `E_COLLECTION_METHOD`. Generic bodies accept the same loops and comprehensions over `[N]T` with a `const N` length.
- [PY-ARRAY-ITER-2] A `for` loop over a fixed array held in a local variable reads the elements from that variable itself when the loop body never writes it — no element write, no reassignment, no destructuring into it and no `mut` argument naming it — so walking the array copies nothing; the loop still visits exactly the value the array had when it began, because nothing changes it. A body that writes the variable, a `mut` array parameter (which may be one of two names for one array), and any other iterable keep the one copy taken when the loop begins.
- [PY-TABLE-1] A dict literal whose expected type is an enum-indexed table `[Kind]T` constructs that table, its keys spelled `Kind.case` and its values arranged in enum layout order independently of source order.
- [PY-TABLE-2] Table literals require exactly one entry per case of their own key enum; missing cases, duplicates, non-case keys and cases of a different enum are rejected.
- [PY-VARIANT-2] Variant construction resolves through the declared type identity, so a local binding shadowing the type name keeps ordinary member resolution and a name that is not a declared case stays an ordinary call. Supplying no payload, more than one payload, a payload to an empty case, or a payload of the wrong type is rejected.
- [PY-VARIANT-4] A union case may declare named fields as `case(f1: T1, f2: T2 = default)`, with the same default-literal and required-after-defaulted rules as record fields. `Type.case(v1, v2)`, `Type.case(f1=v1)`, and positional-then-named mixing construct it, omitting a defaulted field fills its literal; a `case: T` payload and a payload-free `case` keep their existing spelling and meaning. `case Type.case(p1, p2)` binds one name per named field (`_` discards that field), a single-payload case still takes one binding, and a payload-free case takes none. Arity, unknown-field, duplicate-field, missing-required-field, pattern-arity and dropped-result diagnostics are located. Exhaustiveness, wildcard arms, result obligations, equality, layout and native lowering behave exactly as for a single payload.


- [PY-COLLECTIONS-1] Managed list literals infer homogeneous element types or use contextual list types; append, nested indexes, receiver expressions, and indexed writes evaluate operands once. Legacy writes retain receiver-first order; Python function writes evaluate the assigned value before the target. Signed negative indexes count from the end.
- [PY-COLLECTIONS-2] List slices copy the selected elements into a fresh managed list, normalize negative endpoints and clamp to bounds. `len(list)` returns signed i64.
- [PY-COLLECTIONS-3] For-each and enumerate evaluate a list once and retain its initial iteration bound, rejecting structural mutation through aliases before the next body or step; range accepts end or start/end. Break and continue retain correct step semantics.

- [PY-COLLECTIONS-4] Contextual nested literals preserve list types across fields and nested indexes.
- [PY-COLLECTIONS-5] Field assignment reconstructs mutable local records or list-slot records with receiver/index operands evaluated once, retaining value semantics for previous copies.
- [PY-COLLECTIONS-6] Generic list literals, iteration, slicing and writes preserve symbolic element types and allocation effects.
- [PY-COLLECTIONS-7] Receiver calls, computed indexes and assignment values run exactly once in source order and survive collection at every allocation.
- [PY-COLLECTIONS-8] Structural list mutation through an alias traps during continued iteration, including continue and the final step; break exits without another iteration check.
- [PY-COLLECTIONS-9] Generic record field updates preserve managed alias copying and concrete value semantics.
- [PY-COLLECTIONS-10] Slicing clamps negative and oversized endpoints, produces empty reversed intervals and copies list storage while retaining shallow element values.

- [PY-COLLECTIONS-11] Contextual types attach to complete expressions so an annotated postfix expression does not incorrectly constrain its receiver literal.
- [PY-COLLECTIONS-12] End-only range syntax starts at signed zero in runtime and compile-time scalar functions.
