A `str` key object's absence is never recorded, because a collected text's address can be reused by an equal text that is present (a described slice's absence is, dated by the heap's collection count — [DICT-RANGE-KEY-2]); the write after a missed `str` read walks the table again, comparing only stored hashes. `setdefault` probes once for either outcome. Lookups, insertion order, defaults, traps and arena ownership are unchanged.
- [PY-DICT-5] Each index slot packs its key's 32-bit hash above one past the entry index it names, still eight bytes per slot, so a probe loads an entry only when the stored hash equals the queried key's and a table grows by re-placing its slots from their stored hashes without reading or rehashing a key. `pop` empties its key's slot by shifting the rest of the collision run back, so every remaining key stays reachable from its home slot, then renumbers the slots of every later entry in place: it allocates nothing, hashes at most the popped key and stays linear in the table's size. A slot names at most 2³²−2 entries; a longer entry list drops the index and scans, exactly as a refused index allocation does.
- [DICT-RANGE-KEY-1] A `str` slice whose value is never anything but the key of a lookup or write on a `str`-keyed dictionary is described instead of built. `d[s[a:b]]`, `d.get(s[a:b], x)`, `s[a:b] in d`, `d[s[a:b]] = v`, `d[s[a:b]] += v`, and a local `key = s[a:b]` read only as such a key — `counts[key] = counts.get(key, 0) + 1`, `counts[key] += 1` — keep the receiver text as the slice's value and, beside it in the frame, the octet offset and length the slice would hold, clamped exactly as the slice clamps its scalar endpoints. The dictionary hashes and compares those octets in place with the hash an owned text of the same octets gets, so a lookup allocates nothing, and a write whose key is new stores one owned key built from them in the dictionary's own region, its hash already cached. The rule is proved per instruction: the value flows, with no branch into the window, straight from the slice (or from a read of such a local) into the next instruction's store into such a local, or into the dictionary operation's key operand with at most two constant or local pushes between; a local qualifies only when nothing else names it. The receiver held in the slot keeps its text alive exactly as long as the key can be read, so roots, frame maps and liveness are unchanged. A program that opens an arena keeps building its slices, so the store barrier traps `R_ARENA_ESCAPE` wherever it did. Values, insertion order, stored keys, defaults and the `R_MISSING_KEY` trap are unchanged; only allocation — and with it collection timing and the memory budget's headroom — changes.
- [DICT-RANGE-KEY-2] A lookup by a described slice that misses records the absence with the text it ranges over, the span and the heap's collection count, so the write of a read-modify-write inserts at the empty slot the lookup ended on without hashing or walking the table again. The record is believed only while no arena is open and no collection has run since it was written — a tracing-heap text is reclaimed only by a collection, so an unchanged count proves the recorded address still names the same immutable text — and every insertion, table change, `pop`, `clear` and reindex drops it first. A recorded found entry answers a described slice only after its stored key's octets compare equal. A slice's recorded absence is kept apart from an ordinary key's, so a lookup of a whole text is never answered by the record of a slice of it.
- [DICT-OVERWRITE-1] A dictionary write — `d[k] = v`, `d[k] += v`, and the write half of `d[k] = d.get(k, 0) + 1`, with any key type and with a described slice as the key ([DICT-RANGE-KEY-1]) — lowers to its allocation-free half first: `overwrite` replaces the value of a key already present and answers whether it was, and only a key it found absent reaches the allocating `write`, whose root push is linked around that call alone. A write of a present key — every write of a counting loop after a key's first — therefore publishes no root, and a miss is recorded exactly as a lookup's, so the `write` that follows inserts at the slot the miss ended on where the key type records absences ([PY-DICT-4], [DICT-RANGE-KEY-2]). Values, insertion order, traps, the store barrier and allocation are unchanged.
- [LIST-SORT-KEY-1] `values.sort(key=f, reverse=b)` sorts a list in place and `sorted(values, key=f, reverse=b)` sorts a copy, with Python's keyword-only `key=` and `reverse=`, both optional. The key maps one element to an ordered key — any integer width, `bool`, `float`, `str`, or a flat tuple of those compared field by field — and is evaluated exactly once per element, in input order, before any element moves. The order is stable: elements with equal keys keep their input order, and `reverse=True` orders the keys descending while equal keys still keep input order, so it is not sort-then-reverse. Float keys order as PY-COLLECTIONS-15 orders floats — signed zeros equal, every NaN after every ordered value — and `reverse=True` puts the NaNs first. Without a key, `values.sort(reverse=True)` and `sorted(values, reverse=True)` order an `int`, `float` or `str` list by its elements under the same rules; a bare `values.sort()` and `sorted(values)` are unchanged. The list keeps its identity, length and capacity, and every alias sees the new order. A key that traps propagates the trap before any element has moved.
- [LIST-SORT-KEY-2] A keyed sort's operands are checked where they are written: a positional operand, an unknown keyword or a repeated one is `E_CALL_ARGUMENT` with the accepted signature, and `cmp=` also names the key alternative; a key that is not a callback of exactly one element of the list's element type, that declares its argument `mut`, or that returns a type with no order is `E_SORT_KEY` naming the types; `reverse=` of any type but `bool` is `E_TYPE`; a sort without a key over an element with no native order is `E_COLLECTION_METHOD`. A lambda key's key type is read off its body when the body is the element, a field path from it, a negation or `len` of one, or a tuple of those, and is otherwise the body's ordinary type (LAMBDA-KEY-INFER-1). The key is invoked like any callback value, so its declared effect ceiling is charged to the enclosing function. Named arguments on any other collection method are `E_CALL_ARGUMENT`.
- [LAMBDA-KEY-INFER-1] A one-parameter sort-key lambda (`sorted(values, key=lambda v: ...)`, `values.sort(key=...)`) whose body is not one of the shapes LIST-SORT-KEY-2 reads takes the body's ordinary checked type as its key type: the body is checked once with its parameter bound to the list's element type and no expected result, and every trace of that trial (typed nodes, statements, locals, resolved entries, the parameter binding, option facts, ownership state and diagnostics) is undone before the lambda is checked for real against the slot `def(T) -> K effects(managed_allocation)` it fixes. The slot admits allocation because its one call site is the keyed sort, which the enclosing function is already charged `managed_allocation` for, so a key that builds a `str` (`p.name.lower()`, a slice, a helper that concatenates) is exact rather than exempt; a declared key function with a narrower ceiling still fills it. So a method call, a call to a declared function, a conversion, a slice or a conditional expression gives a `str`, `float` or integer key as written, where it used to be typed `int` and fail with `expected int, found str`. A body that does not type on its own is one `E_SORT_KEY` at the lambda that quotes the underlying diagnostic and says to write a top-level key function with a declared result; a key type with no order is still LIST-SORT-KEY-2's `E_SORT_KEY`.
- [LIST-SORT-KEY-3] A keyed sort lowers to one key list sized to the list, filled by one pass that calls the key once per element, and one native `myl_native_list_sort_keyed` call. A numeric key becomes an order-preserving 64-bit word — the sign bit flipped, binary64 bits reflected with signed zeros merged and every NaN the largest word, all complemented for `reverse` — and when the words span fewer distinct values than there are elements, a stable counting sort gives each element, in input order, the next slot of its key; otherwise, when every word minus the smallest fits above the position bits, `(word - smallest) << bits | position` words are sorted by the integer engine in the key list's own storage; keys spread wider take two such sorts of 32 bits each. Text and tuple keys sort (key, position) entries with a stable merge. For `values.sort(...)` the elements then move once along the permutation's cycles through element-sized temporaries, prefetching a few steps ahead on each cycle, so the extra memory is the key list plus the sort's scratch and no second list of elements exists; for `sorted(values, ...)` they are gathered in order from the original list into a new list allocated at the list's length, so the copy is never built only to be permuted. The native sort calls no guest code and allocates nothing managed; scratch memory the host refuses is the allocation-failure trap, raised before any element moves.
- [QBE-INVOKE-ROOTS-1] A call through a callback value is a safepoint: the caller pushes every live traced local and operand as a root before the indirect call, exactly as it does before a direct call to a function that can allocate, because the callee it reaches is known only at run time and may collect. Before this, a key or other callback that allocated could let a collection free objects that only the caller's locals held.
- [SYNTAX-METHOD-KEYWORDS-1] A method call on an expression receiver, `holder.items.sort(key=f)`, records a keyword for each named argument exactly as the named-receiver form `items.sort(key=f)` does, with the same rule that positional arguments precede keyword arguments.

## Local-reasoning syntax foundation

- [SYNTAX-MUT-1] `mut` marks a written value on both sides of a call: `name: mut T` in a declared parameter list sets `Parameter.mut` and leaves `ty` the span of `T` alone, `mut argument` wraps the argument expression in a `mut_argument` node the checker may accept or reject positionally, and `def(values: mut list[int]) -> None` records one Boolean per parameter in `CallbackType.mut_parameters` in source order. `mut` is matched by the text of an identifier and never by a lexer tag, so it stays an ordinary name as a parameter, local, field, function or argument; and the parser does not validate a `mut` target. The checker turns the declaration side into the mutation footprint and enforces the argument side, which [MUT-PARAM-1] specifies.
- [SYNTAX-KEYWORD-ONLY-1] A bare `*` in a declared function's parameter list sets `FunctionSignature.keyword_only_from` to the zero-based index of the first parameter after it, which is also the positional parameter count, and is null when the list has no `*`. Only the positional group keeps the required-before-defaulted rule, so a required keyword-only parameter may follow a defaulted one. A `*` with no following parameter is `E_SYNTAX` at the `*` naming the missing parameter, a second `*` in one list is `E_SYNTAX` at that `*`, and a `*` in a callback type is `E_SYNTAX` because a callback slot is positional. Record field lists and lambdas gain nothing.
- [SYNTAX-TUPLE-1] A parenthesized comma list builds a `tuple` node whose element nodes are in source order, and a trailing comma after two or more elements is accepted; a parenthesized expression with no comma stays that expression, so `(a)` is `a`. A bare comma list builds the same node after `return` and on both sides of a multi-target assignment, where the target list is a `tuple` of `name` nodes and the value is the right-hand `tuple`, reached through the same assignment node inside a discard that `rec.field = value` uses. One element is never a tuple: `(a,)`, `x, = pair` and a bare comma list with no `=` are `E_SYNTAX`.
- [SYNTAX-TUPLE-2] `tuple[T1, ..., Tn]` in any type position appends one `TupleType` with the element spans to `TypeExpressions.tuples`, from the `tuple` keyword through the closing bracket. The parser accepts any comma count, including one, so arity outside two to eight is a checker rule (`E_TUPLE_ARITY`) rather than a parse error, and `tuple` stays an ordinary type name when no bracket follows it.
- [SYNTAX-CONDITIONAL-1] `value_if_true if condition else value_if_false` parses as a `conditional` node and binds looser than every binary operator, with its condition parsed above `or` and its else arm at the lowest precedence, so `a or b if p else c` takes `a or b` as its then-arm and `a if p else b if q else c` puts a nested conditional in its else arm. A `kw_if` that begins a new logical line still starts an `if` statement, never a conditional.
- [SYNTAX-CONDITIONAL-2] A conditional expression is accepted in every expression position — a call argument, a keyword-argument value, a list literal element, an f-string hole, a `return` value and an assignment value — because it is parsed by the one expression entry point.
- [SYNTAX-FMT-1] The token formatter prints every new spelling through its ordinary spacing rules, so `mylang fmt` returns them unchanged: one space around `mut` and around a bare `*`, no space before a comma and one after it in a tuple or a `tuple[...]` type, and one space around `if` and `else` in a conditional. A trailing comma before a closing delimiter is the one exception: canonical text drops it consistently, in tuples as in argument and field lists, which never changes a program's meaning because no one-element tuple is spellable.
- [MUT-PARAM-1] A parameter a function writes through is declared `name: mut T`, and those positions are the function's declared mutation footprint (`FunctionSignature.mut_positions` on the AST, `typed.FunctionSignature.declared_mutates`), replacing the retired `mutates(...)` clause. The marker is required exactly where the callee declares it — an argument bound to one is written `mut argument`, and a call that omits it is `E_MUTATION_MARKER` naming the paste `f(mut x, …)` and the position. A `mut` marker on a position the callee does not write is `E_MUTATION_MARKER` too, rejected at the argument's own span with a message naming the function and the argument and saying to remove `mut`. The rule applies to resolved direct calls and to calls through callable values, whose `mut` parameters come from the callback type in the same spelling, and a receiver is never marked: `xs.append(v)` and `counts.sort()` need nothing.
- [MUT-PARAM-2] On the declaration side the same error covers both directions. A parameter the body writes with no `mut` is `E_MUTATION_MARKER` at the parameter, whose message lists each written parameter by name and position and gives the exact declaration to paste (`values: mut list[i64]`); a parameter declared `mut` that the body never writes is `E_MUTATION_MARKER` naming the parameter and `remove `mut``. Forwarding a parameter as `mut` to another call counts as a write, so a wrapper that only forwards is correctly `mut`, and a parameter whose only writes are provably fresh local storage is not a write at all. A `match` payload binding takes the origin of the value it was matched from, so a write through `case Some(items): items.append(v)` — or through a narrowed `Option` parameter, `candidate.field = v` after `if candidate is None: return` — is a write to that parameter and needs its `mut`.
- [MUT-PARAM-3] `mut` is accepted only on a managed-bearing parameter type — `list`, `dict`, `class`, `Cell`, `[N]T`, `byte_buffer`, and a `record`, union, option or result holding one, which is the same definition arenas use. A scalar, `str`, `bytes` or scalar-only record parameter declared `mut` rejects with `E_MUTATION_MARKER` (`mut` on `int` has nothing to write…), because no write through it could reach the caller. A `mut` argument must be assignable: a variable, field or indexed slot rooted at one, so `f(mut [1, 2])` is `E_MUTATION_MARKER` whose message says to assign it to a local first.
- [PY-ARRAY-MUT-1] `a: mut [N]T` (and `mut [Kind]T`) is accepted for every element type that is copyable without a pending result, and makes the parameter name the caller's own array for the whole call: `a[i] = v` in the callee writes the caller's variable, and so does passing `mut a` on to another such parameter. The caller writes `f(mut a)` under the ordinary marker rules, and the argument must be a mutable variable — a local array or a `mut` array parameter being passed on; a field or element is `E_MUTATION_MARKER` saying to copy it into a local, pass that and store it back. An element write through an array parameter that is not `mut`, directly or by passing it as `mut`, is `E_MUTATION_MARKER` naming the parameter, exactly as a list parameter's write is, and a `mut` array parameter the body never writes is the same unused-`mut` error. Rebinding a `mut` array parameter as a whole (`a = other`, `a = replace(a, i, v)`, a destructuring target) is `E_ASSIGNMENT_TARGET`, because the name is the caller's array. `f(mut a, a)` gives the read-only parameter a snapshot taken at the call, and `f(mut a, mut a)` is two writable names for one array written in sequence. A function with a `mut` array parameter cannot be a function value (`E_CALLBACK_TYPE`) and a lambda cannot fill a callback with one (`E_LAMBDA_CONTEXT`), and a host or C ABI function cannot declare one. Whole reassignment of an ordinary array parameter, which never reaches the caller, is unchanged.
- [MUT-PARAM-4] Overlap and permission are unchanged by the new spelling: a declared `mut` footprint still rejects a writable argument that may alias a read-only one with `E_MUTATION_OVERLAP` and a write that exceeds the declared effect ceiling with `E_MUTATION_PERMISSION`, and passing `mut x` where `x` is the caller's own parameter requires that parameter to be `mut` through the same footprint substitution. Fresh local storage is exempt from the overlap proof only against caller-owned inputs: two arguments that read the same fresh local, or locals assigned one from another or bound to a `match` payload of one, may alias, so `touch(mut values, values)` rejects, while a `copy()` is a different object and does not. A `mut` parameter still contributes `mutation` to the declared-ceiling check.
- [MUT-OVERLAP-PROOF-1] The disjointness proof behind `E_MUTATION_OVERLAP` is a reachability search over unordered pairs of types: starting from the writable and the read-only argument types, a pair is replaced by either side's managed components (list and dictionary elements, array elements, domain backings, union payloads and fields), and the arguments may alias exactly when some reachable pair is one type, one nominal record, or a record the catalog cannot describe. Each pair is expanded at most once per query, so the cost is polynomial in the number of reachable types: two families of mutually recursive classes check at once where the per-path walk it replaces never finished, and a shared component anywhere in either graph still rejects the call.
- [MUT-TRACK-1] Mutation tracking follows every binding that can alias a parameter's storage. A `match` arm's payload binding aliases the matched value when the payload carries writable storage (a class, a list), so a write through the payload, or through a local it is copied into, writes whatever owns the matched value: a `close(rt: mut Runtime, i)` that closes an upvalue taken from `match rt.objects[i]` writes `rt`, and the same function without `mut` is `E_MUTATION_MARKER`; the overlap check sees the same origin. Binding origins are computed to a fixed point over the whole body, so a binding that a later statement in a loop widens still carries the widened origin at every write. The missing-marker diagnostic points at the first write that reaches a parameter not declared `mut`, never at a write of a declared one.
- [MUT-PARAM-5] With `mut` supplying the footprint, the `mutates(...)` clause and the opt-in `&` call-argument marker are removed spellings: a clause is `E_REMOVED_SPELLING` at `mutates` saying “mark each written parameter `name: mut T`”, `&` is `E_REMOVED_SPELLING` saying to write `mut value`, and the `--mutation-markers` switch is rejected on every command. The reference-typed `class` exemption and the advisory `W_MUTATES_MISSING` warning are gone with them: a write through a class parameter now needs its own `mut` like every other position, and the check is an error, not a warning.
- [MUT-PARAM-6] Every signature surface renders the new spelling: `list-interfaces`, `context --symbol`, `schema-project` and `api-diff` print `name: mut T` in the signature text (so `interface_sha256` covers it), the separate `mutates(...)` column is dropped from the interface listing while the published `mutates` field name and its position list stay for consumers, and `mylang fmt` returns `name: mut T` and `mut argument` unchanged.
- [KEYWORD-ONLY-1] A parameter declared after a bare `*` binds by name only: a call that supplies one positionally is `E_KEYWORD_ONLY`, reported at the callee, naming that parameter and printing the whole call rewritten with every keyword-only operand passed by name (`io.issue(host, code=13, file=0, position=span.start)`). The repair is rendered from the call site's own source text by the one call renderer the call-site rejections use, so callee, operand order and operand spelling are the ones written. A required keyword-only parameter that no operand supplies keeps the ordinary `E_ARITY`, and binding by name in any order, mixed with positional operands, is unchanged.
- [KEYWORD-ONLY-2] The keyword-only boundary is part of the declaration, not of a call: required and defaulted keyword-only parameters may mix in any order, an omitted defaulted keyword-only parameter takes its declared default, and the positional group keeps the required-before-defaulted rule. `keyword_only_from` reaches binding through the declared `FunctionSignature`, so every call site that binds a declared function — an ordinary call, a compile-time `const` call and a generic body — enforces the same rule.
- [KEYWORD-ONLY-3] The rendered interface signature carries the `*`: `list-interfaces` prints `def(host:Host,*,code:int)->i64` for a declaration that makes `code` keyword-only, so the published contract and the call-site rejection agree on which parameters are positional.
- [CONDITIONAL-EXPR-1] `value_if_true if condition else value_if_false` is an expression whose condition must be `bool` and whose two arms have one type: the contextual expected type when the destination supplies one, and otherwise the then arm's type, with an integer literal arm adopting the expected width and no numeric widening between arms. Arms that disagree are `E_TYPE` `conditional arms must agree: \`int\` here, \`str\` in the else arm`, reported at the first arm; a non-`bool` condition is the ordinary `E_TYPE` `expected bool, found int`.
- [CONDITIONAL-EXPR-2] A conditional is one expression wherever an expression is accepted: a `return` value, an assignment or binding value, a call or keyword-argument value, a list or record-constructor element, an f-string hole, and the else arm of another conditional (which is why `a if p else b if q else c` nests to the right). `Some(x) if p else None` and `Ok(x) if p else Err(e)` take their arm type from the expected `Option` or `Result`, exactly as those constructors do in any other contextual position.
- [CONDITIONAL-EXPR-3] Only the chosen arm is evaluated, so `a if p else f()` never calls `f` when `p` holds and an unchosen arm that would trap does not. Both arms are still checked: each contributes its reads (including `E_UNBOUND` for a name no path reaching it assigns), its effects and its origins, and a standalone conditional whose arms are not unit keeps `E_EXPLICIT_DISCARD_REQUIRED` like any other non-unit expression statement.
- [CONDITIONAL-EXPR-4] The check lowers the conditional to the if/else the rest of the compiler already understands — a fresh unnamed temporary that each arm assigns and the block reads — so no new checked node, effect rule, origin rule or bytecode shape is introduced, and a conditional inside a generic function body is checked symbolically with the same arm agreement and condition rules.
- [KEYWORD-ONLY-4] The `positional-literal` lint (report-only, `L_POSITIONAL_LITERAL`) reports the last operand a call of three or more arguments passes positionally as a `bool`, integer or `None` literal, at the callee: `` `True` is passed positionally to `draw`'s 4th parameter `filled`; name it: `draw(canvas, x=10, y=20, filled=True)` ``. The reported parameter is the one that operand binds to, an intrinsic or a call through a callback value is exempt because it has no parameter to name, and the repaired call comes from the shared call renderer with every operand from the first positional literal on written by name, so the paste is a call that binds.
- [TUPLE-1] A tuple is an anonymous record interned in the record catalog, keyed by its element types in source order, with positional fields named `$0`, `$1`, … that no source can spell; a module program keeps every `tuple[...]` spelling through the merge that combines its units, so a tuple type is one identity in a single file and in a project alike; the element list always interns to one identity, whether it is reached from a `tuple[T1, ..., Tn]` spelling through the layout graph or from a value through `record_layouts.tupleRecord`, so `tuple[int, str]` is one type in signatures, comparisons, caches and interface rendering. A spelling outside the two-to-eight element range is `E_TUPLE_ARITY` at the whole `tuple[...]` spelling, and a tuple is a plain record everywhere else: construction is a record construct, a read is a positional field read, and the contract of the whole (storable, move-only, result-handling, managed-bearing) is the element-wise rule `record_layouts` already derives from fields.
- [TUPLE-2] `(a, b)` and the bare forms after `return` build a tuple value from elements checked in source order, with the expected-type rule for literals: when the destination already demands `tuple[T1, T2]` each element is checked against its positional type, and otherwise the element types are inferred and interned. A tuple may be stored in a list, as a dictionary value, in a record field, in an `Option` or `Result` payload, and nested in another tuple; it is managed-bearing exactly when one of its elements is, and a value whose arity differs from the demanded tuple type is `E_TUPLE_ARITY` naming the count it has and the count it needs.
- [TUPLE-3] `x, y = value` destructures a tuple: the value is evaluated once into one temporary, its arity is checked against the number of names (`E_TUPLE_ARITY` naming the source of the value and the number of names to bind), and each name is bound to the positional field of that temporary, so `a, b = b, a` swaps with no intermediate list string. `_` in a target list binds nothing and reads nothing, a name may be rebound by the assignment like any other assignment, and a right-hand side that is not a tuple is `E_TYPE` naming the single-binding alternative.
- [PY-DESTRUCTURE-PLACE-1] A destructuring target list may name elements and fields as well as names: `a[i], a[j] = a[j], a[i]` swaps two elements of a fixed array or list, and `d[k], r.f, xs[-1].g, name = value` writes a dictionary entry, a record field, a field of an element and a binding. The value is evaluated once, before any target, into one temporary whose arity is checked as `TUPLE-3` says; then each target in order evaluates its own receiver and indices and takes its element exactly as `target = element` would, with the same type, mutability, `mut`-parameter, arena and negative-index rules. A target that is not a name, element or field is `E_ASSIGNMENT_TARGET`.
- [TUPLE-4] `for number, letter in pairs` destructures each two-element tuple element through the same two-binding path `DictItem`, `zip` and `enumerate` already use, so the loop variable names stay scoped to the body and the element type is the tuple's two positional types; a tuple element of any other arity is `E_TUPLE_ARITY` naming the one-name alternative.
- [TUPLE-5] A tuple has no elements to index, no fields, no methods and no `len`: `pair[0]` is `E_TUPLE_INDEX` naming the destructuring alternative, `pair.head` and `pair.count()` are `E_TYPE` naming the same alternative, and `len(pair)` is `E_TYPE` naming it too. A tuple type compared against a different tuple type is `E_TYPE` through the ordinary expected/found message, dictionary keys stay `int` and `str` so a tuple is never a key, `DictItem`, `zip` and `enumerate` are unchanged, `match` patterns over tuples are not accepted, and the host and JSON boundary rejects a tuple graph like any other non-data graph.
- [TUPLE-6] A tuple prints as `(1, 'a')` — parenthesized, comma-space separated, each element rendered exactly as `print` renders that element alone, so a text element is repr-quoted — and `==` on two tuples follows the aggregate rule: structural, element-wise and recursive, comparing text content rather than reference.
- [TUPLE-7] A tuple type renders in its source spelling, `tuple[int, str]`: `mylang fmt` returns a tuple value `(a, b)`, a `tuple[...]` type and a destructuring target list unchanged, and `list-interfaces` renders a signature that names a tuple through the canonical catalog name `tuple<i64,str>`, which the same surface rewriter turns back into `tuple[int, str]`.
- [TUPLE-8] A tuple type in a template keeps its elements abstract: `tuple[T, int]` is the ordered argument list of the anonymous tuple declaration, not a representative concrete type, so a generic function may take, return and destructure such a tuple and the bound element keeps its parameter identity. Requirements on a template tuple are its elements' requirements rather than a declaration-level guarantee, and a fully concrete element list materializes the same interned record that a closed `tuple[...]` spelling does, so a generic body and its specializations agree on identity.
- [OPTION-METHODS-1] `Option[T]` answers three intrinsic methods and nothing else: `unwrap_or(default: T) -> T`, `is_none() -> bool` and `is_some() -> bool`. They are reachable on both receiver spellings — a named local, whose call is checked where the collection receiver arm runs, and any other expression, whose method call is checked on the `E_COLLECTION_METHOD` dispatch path — and `unwrap_or`'s default is checked against the payload type with the ordinary expected-type rule, so `head(narrow).unwrap_or(7)` adopts a `u8` payload and a mismatched default reports `E_TYPE` with `` `unwrap_or` default must be `int` (the Option's payload), found str ``. All three are pure, allocate nothing, and lower through `option_flow.zig`'s existing exhaustive variant dispatch: presence tests to the tag test and `unwrap_or` to a two-arm match that roots the receiver, evaluates the default eagerly into a fresh temporary and binds either the payload or that temporary into one result slot.
- [OPTION-METHODS-2] A generic body accepts the same three methods through the symbolic checker, so a template whose parameter is an `Option[T]` may call `unwrap_or`, `is_none` and `is_some` without an instantiation: the default is checked against the symbolic payload and the method contributes no effect, so a `noalloc` ceiling accepts every one of them. An `Option` carrying an owned value or a checked result still requires explicit `match`, exactly as a presence test does, and a receiver that has no such method reports `E_COLLECTION_METHOD` naming `unwrap_or(default)`, `is_none()` and `is_some()` rather than the collection vocabulary.
- [OPTION-METHODS-3] The `sentinel-int` lint reports an `int` used as a sentinel for the absent case: a function whose declared result is an integer and which returns a negative integer literal on some path, where a local bound from that call (or the call itself) is compared against a negative literal — or the `< 0`/`>= 0` presence guards — is reported at that comparison with `` `find_pin` returns -1 on one path and its result is tested against a negative literal here; return `Option[int]` and test `is None` or use `unwrap_or` ``, and a record field or a parameter compared against `-1` in two or more places is reported once with the same rewrite. The rule is `L_SENTINEL_INT`, report-only under `agent` and blocking under `strict` and `safety`; it is exempt by name for `find` and `find_any`, whose `-1` is their own contract.
- [MUT-PARAM-7] A `mut` argument marker inside a generic function body is transparent to the symbolic checker: the marked value is checked with the type it would have unmarked, so a template that forwards `mut` to a declared `mut` parameter checks and its concrete specializations re-check every call with the declared footprint and full origin analysis. The marker therefore has one spelling and one meaning in an ordinary body and in a template alike.

- [DERIVED-EFFECTS-1] A function's effect ceiling is derived from its parameter list: a `Stream` parameter permits `output`, `Filesystem` `filesystem`, `Network` `network`, `Clock` `clock`, `Entropy` `random`, `Environment` `environment`, `Host` `host`, a `mut` parameter `mutation`, a callback parameter the bounded ceiling its type declares, and `managed_allocation` is always permitted; the ownership a parameter or the result type carries contributes the effect of the resource family it holds, because a value the body may drop is authority the signature already grants. A body that exceeds that ceiling is rejected in that body with `E_EFFECT_BOUND` at the operation or call that reaches the effect, and the message names the parameter to add (`add `out: Stream` to the parameters`). A `host def` or `extern def` has no body to derive from, so its clause stays its ceiling as written, and a callback type whose ceiling is omitted is unbounded rather than a promise: it contributes nothing to the derived set and an invoke through such a value contributes nothing either, because the value's own signature is where that authority is declared.
- [DERIVED-EFFECTS-2] `noalloc`, `noio` and `effects(...)` survive only as narrowings of the derived ceiling. A clause that permits exactly what the parameters imply — `effects(host, managed_allocation)` on a `Host` parameter, or `noio` on a body with no io-capable parameter — is `W_DERIVED_EFFECTS` at the clause, whose message quotes the clause and says to remove it; `noalloc` never restates, because allocation is always permitted. The warning blocks under `lint --profile=strict`, where it is the `derived-effects` rule.
- [DERIVED-EFFECTS-3] A clause that names an effect no parameter can supply is `E_DERIVED_EFFECTS` at the clause, because it can never be satisfied: the message lists every unsupplied effect and the parameter paste that would supply each one (`add `fs: Filesystem` to the parameters or drop the clause`). Only an explicit `effects(...)` list can name one; `noalloc` and `noio` withhold effects instead and are checked only for restating the derived set.
- [DERIVED-EFFECTS-4] The published `ceiling` is the derived set narrowed by an explicit clause, for a declaration whose body was never checked as much as for a checked one: `list-interfaces` prints `ceiling(host,managed_allocation)` from the parameter list alone beside `analysis(declaration-only)`, the contract manifest's `permissions` and the public schema publish it, and `interface_sha256` carries it in place of the inferred mask. The published `effects` fact stays the checked body fact, so a body-only edit that adds an allocation inside the derived ceiling changes `body_sha256` and leaves every declaration interface and caller type check untouched.
- [UNUSED-CAPABILITY-1] A capability parameter the body never reads is `E_UNUSED_CAPABILITY` at the parameter, replacing the `W_UNUSED_BINDING` it used to be: the message names the parameter and says the signature claims an effect the body cannot have (`remove the parameter or use it`). Reading it at all counts as use, including handing it to another call, so a frame that only forwards its `Host` keeps it; `Stream` and `Host` are covered like every other capability, and a `host def`/`extern def` declaration, which has no body, is exempt.

## Self-hosting oracle

- [SELFHOST-LEXER-DUMP-1] `mylang dump-tokens <file.my>` lexes exactly one file and writes a single-line `tokens-v1` JSON document to stdout with the compiler's own token stream: a `version` string, the `path`, a `count`, and one record per token in source order carrying the exact `@tagName` kind spelling, the half-open byte `start`/`end`, and 1-based `line`/`column`. It stops at the lexer — it never loads imports, parses, checks or lowers — and it is the stage-0 reference a differential self-host lexer is compared against. A lexical failure prints the ordinary diagnostics to stderr, writes no partial document, and exits 1.
